What finally made me come in is that the moment my car reaches one of those long bridges toward Miami, my chest just tightens up. My hands clamp down on the steering wheel, and I get this sudden, overwhelming certainty that I have to get off the bridge right then—even though there’s obviously nowhere to go. It just hits me all at once.\n\nDoctor: Is it just bridges that cause this reaction, or are there other situations or places that trigger a similar feeling?\n\nPatient: No, it really just seems to be the bridges. I mean, I drive around town for my errands or to see friends, and I'm perfectly fine. It's only when I have to cross those long stretches over the water. I haven't had that feeling happen anywhere else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you might be having a panic attack when this happens on the bridge?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. I don't know exactly what qualifies as a panic attack, to be honest. I just know that my chest gets tight and I get that overwhelming urge to get away, like I mentioned. But as for whether it's an actual panic attack... I couldn't say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode like the one you described on the bridge in any other situation?\n\nPatient: No, I really haven't. Like I mentioned a moment ago, it's... it's strictly the bridges. I've never felt that sudden tightness or that overwhelming need to escape anywhere else. Whether I'm at home, or just out shopping, I feel completely normal. It only ever happens when I'm driving over the water on those long stretches.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any other situations where you feel like you're having a panic attack?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't. Like I said, it's just the bridges. I don't have those feelings anywhere else, just when I'm driving over the water.\n\nDoctor: Is it the height of the bridges that scares you, or is it more the fact that you're on the bridge itself, regardless of how high it is?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure, to be honest. I haven't really thought about it quite like that. I just know that... well, when I'm out there on the bridge, especially over the water, that's when that feeling hits me. I couldn't really tell you if the height itself is what's doing it, or just the fact that I'm stuck out there on it. I just haven't noticed if a lower bridge feels any different than a high one.\n\nDoctor: Is it the idea of being on a bridge itself that scares you, or is there something else about the situation that you think might be contributing to your fear?\n\nPatient: I think it's just the bridge itself, and being over all that open water. I mean, I know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years. Plenty of people cross them every day without a second thought. But... when I think about driving over them, or when I'm actually doing it, the fear I feel just seems so much bigger than the actual danger. I know I'm safe, logically, but my body just doesn't believe it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel the same level of fear if you were walking on the bridge rather than driving on it?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, honestly. I've never tried walking across one of those bridges... I mean, they're highway bridges, mostly, so you can't exactly walk on them anyway. But even if I could, I think... well, I imagine I'd probably still be just as terrified. It's being out there over the water that gets me, so I don't think it would matter if I was in a car or on my own two feet.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.231, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm here because I'm having a lot of trouble with driving lately. Specifically, it's the bridges. What finally made me come in is that the moment my car reaches one of those long bridges toward Miami, my chest just tightens up. My hands clamp down on the steering wheel, and I get this sudden, overwhelming certainty that I have to get off the bridge right then—even though there’s obviously nowhere to go. It just hits me all at once.\n\nDoctor: Is it just bridges that cause this reaction, or are there other situations or places that trigger a similar feeling?\n\nPatient: No, it really just seems to be the bridges. I mean, I drive around town for my errands or to see friends, and I'm perfectly fine. It's only when I have to cross those long stretches over the water. I haven't had that feeling happen anywhere else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you might be having a panic attack when this happens on the bridge?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. I don't know exactly what qualifies as a panic attack, to be honest. I just know that my chest gets tight and I get that overwhelming urge to get away, like I mentioned. But as for whether it's an actual panic attack... I couldn't say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode like the one you described on the bridge in any other situation?\n\nPatient: No, I really haven't. Like I mentioned a moment ago, it's... it's strictly the bridges. I've never felt that sudden tightness or that overwhelming need to escape anywhere else. Whether I'm at home, or just out shopping, I feel completely normal. It only ever happens when I'm driving over the water on those long stretches.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any other situations where you feel like you're having a panic attack?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't. Like I said, it's just the bridges. I don't have those feelings anywhere else, just when I'm driving over the water.\n\nDoctor: Is it the height of the bridges that scares you, or is it more the fact that you're on the bridge itself, regardless of how high it is?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure, to be honest. I haven't really thought about it quite like that. I just know that... well, when I'm out there on the bridge, especially over the water, that's when that feeling hits me. I couldn't really tell you if the height itself is what's doing it, or just the fact that I'm stuck out there on it. I just haven't noticed if a lower bridge feels any different than a high one.\n\nDoctor: Is it the idea of being on a bridge itself that scares you, or is there something else about the situation that you think might be contributing to your fear?\n\nPatient: I think it's just the bridge itself, and being over all that open water. I mean, I know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years. Plenty of people cross them every day without a second thought. But... when I think about driving over them, or when I'm actually doing it, the fear I feel just seems so much bigger than the actual danger. I know I'm safe, logically, but my body just doesn't believe it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel the same level of fear if you were walking on the bridge rather than driving on it?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, honestly. I've never tried walking across one of those bridges... I mean, they're highway bridges, mostly, so you can't exactly walk on them anyway. But even if I could, I think... well, I imagine I'd probably still be just as terrified. It's being out there over the water that gets me, so I don't think it would matter if I was in a car or on my own two feet.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious or trapped in other situations where you couldn't easily escape, such as in a crowded room or on a plane?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't have any trouble with things like that. I've flown on planes and been in crowded rooms plenty of times in my life, and it's never bothered me. I don't feel trapped in those places at all. It really is just the bridges.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your fear is about not being able to get off the bridge if you needed to, rather than the bridge itself?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think it's about being trapped or not being able to leave. If I were stuck in a car somewhere else, like in a normal traffic jam on solid ground, I wouldn't be afraid at all. It really is just the bridge itself... just being up high over all that water. That's what terrifies me, not the idea of being stuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would decrease if you were driving over a bridge that had a shoulder or an emergency lane where you could pull over if needed?\n\nPatient: I don't really think it would make a difference. Even if there was a place to pull over, I'd still be up there on the bridge, over the water. That's the part that scares me. So, no, I don't think having an emergency lane would help at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you were a passenger in a car driving over a bridge, rather than the one driving?\n\nPatient: I don't think it makes a difference. I... I'd still be just as anxious. Whether I'm driving or someone else is behind the wheel, I'm still in the car, up on that bridge. So, yes, I would definitely still be afraid.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious if you were on a bridge, but not in a car, such as if you were walking or biking across it?\n\nPatient: Well, at eighty-six, I certainly wouldn't be biking. And I haven't tried walking across a big bridge in... well, I don't know how long. So I haven't really been in that situation to know for sure. But just thinking about it—being out there in the open, up that high over the water without even the car around me... I imagine I would be just as terrified, if not more so.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I... I'd still be just as anxious. Whether I'm driving or someone else is behind the wheel, I'm still in the car, up on that bridge. So, yes, I would definitely still be afraid.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious if you were on a bridge, but not in a car, such as if you were walking or biking across it?\n\nPatient: Well, at eighty-six, I certainly wouldn't be biking. And I haven't tried walking across a big bridge in... well, I don't know how long. So I haven't really been in that situation to know for sure. But just thinking about it—being out there in the open, up that high over the water without even the car around me... I imagine I would be just as terrified, if not more so.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould your fear of bridges be less if someone else was driving you across?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 86 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.231, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm here because I'm having a lot of trouble with driving lately. Specifically, it's the bridges. What finally made me come in is that the moment my car reaches one of those long bridges toward Miami, my chest just tightens up. My hands clamp down on the steering wheel, and I get this sudden, overwhelming certainty that I have to get off the bridge right then—even though there’s obviously nowhere to go. It just hits me all at once.\n\nDoctor: Is it just bridges that cause this reaction, or are there other situations or places that trigger a similar feeling?\n\nPatient: No, it really just seems to be the bridges. I mean, I drive around town for my errands or to see friends, and I'm perfectly fine. It's only when I have to cross those long stretches over the water. I haven't had that feeling happen anywhere else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you might be having a panic attack when this happens on the bridge?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. I don't know exactly what qualifies as a panic attack, to be honest. I just know that my chest gets tight and I get that overwhelming urge to get away, like I mentioned. But as for whether it's an actual panic attack... I couldn't say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode like the one you described on the bridge in any other situation?\n\nPatient: No, I really haven't. Like I mentioned a moment ago, it's... it's strictly the bridges. I've never felt that sudden tightness or that overwhelming need to escape anywhere else. Whether I'm at home, or just out shopping, I feel completely normal. It only ever happens when I'm driving over the water on those long stretches.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any other situations where you feel like you're having a panic attack?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't. Like I said, it's just the bridges. I don't have those feelings anywhere else, just when I'm driving over the water.\n\nDoctor: Is it the height of the bridges that scares you, or is it more the fact that you're on the bridge itself, regardless of how high it is?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure, to be honest. I haven't really thought about it quite like that. I just know that... well, when I'm out there on the bridge, especially over the water, that's when that feeling hits me. I couldn't really tell you if the height itself is what's doing it, or just the fact that I'm stuck out there on it. I just haven't noticed if a lower bridge feels any different than a high one.\n\nDoctor: Is it the idea of being on a bridge itself that scares you, or is there something else about the situation that you think might be contributing to your fear?\n\nPatient: I think it's just the bridge itself, and being over all that open water. I mean, I know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years. Plenty of people cross them every day without a second thought. But... when I think about driving over them, or when I'm actually doing it, the fear I feel just seems so much bigger than the actual danger. I know I'm safe, logically, but my body just doesn't believe it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel the same level of fear if you were walking on the bridge rather than driving on it?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, honestly. I've never tried walking across one of those bridges... I mean, they're highway bridges, mostly, so you can't exactly walk on them anyway. But even if I could, I think... well, I imagine I'd probably still be just as terrified. It's being out there over the water that gets me, so I don't think it would matter if I was in a car or on my own two feet.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious or trapped in other situations where you couldn't easily escape, such as in a crowded room or on a plane?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't have any trouble with things like that. I've flown on planes and been in crowded rooms plenty of times in my life, and it's never bothered me. I don't feel trapped in those places at all. It really is just the bridges.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your fear is about not being able to get off the bridge if you needed to, rather than the bridge itself?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think it's about being trapped or not being able to leave. If I were stuck in a car somewhere else, like in a normal traffic jam on solid ground, I wouldn't be afraid at all. It really is just the bridge itself... just being up high over all that water. That's what terrifies me, not the idea of being stuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would decrease if you were driving over a bridge that had a shoulder or an emergency lane where you could pull over if needed?\n\nPatient: I don't really think it would make a difference. Even if there was a place to pull over, I'd still be up there on the bridge, over the water. That's the part that scares me. So, no, I don't think having an emergency lane would help at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you were a passenger in a car driving over a bridge, rather than the one driving?\n\nPatient: I don't think it makes a difference. I... I'd still be just as anxious. Whether I'm driving or someone else is behind the wheel, I'm still in the car, up on that bridge. So, yes, I would definitely still be afraid.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious if you were on a bridge, but not in a car, such as if you were walking or biking across it?\n\nPatient: Well, at eighty-six, I certainly wouldn't be biking. And I haven't tried walking across a big bridge in... well, I don't know how long. So I haven't really been in that situation to know for sure. But just thinking about it—being out there in the open, up that high over the water without even the car around me... I imagine I would be just as terrified, if not more so.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of bridges be less if someone else was driving you across?\n\nPatient: I think we actually just touched on that a moment ago, doctor. But no, it wouldn't be any less. Like I was saying, even if someone else is driving me, I'm still stuck in that car, up high over the water. I haven't really thought about it quite like that. I just know that... well, when I'm out there on the bridge, especially over the water, that's when that feeling hits me. I couldn't really tell you if the height itself is what's doing it, or just the fact that I'm stuck out there on it. I just haven't noticed if a lower bridge feels any different than a high one.\n\nDoctor: Is it the idea of being on a bridge itself that scares you, or is there something else about the situation that you think might be contributing to your fear?\n\nPatient: I think it's just the bridge itself, and being over all that open water. I mean, I know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years. Plenty of people cross them every day without a second thought. But... when I think about driving over them, or when I'm actually doing it, the fear I feel just seems so much bigger than the actual danger. I know I'm safe, logically, but my body just doesn't believe it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel the same level of fear if you were walking on the bridge rather than driving on it?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, honestly. I've never tried walking across one of those bridges... I mean, they're highway bridges, mostly, so you can't exactly walk on them anyway. But even if I could, I think... well, I imagine I'd probably still be just as terrified. It's being out there over the water that gets me, so I don't think it would matter if I was in a car or on my own two feet.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious or trapped in other situations where you couldn't easily escape, such as in a crowded room or on a plane?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't have any trouble with things like that. I've flown on planes and been in crowded rooms plenty of times in my life, and it's never bothered me. I don't feel trapped in those places at all. It really is just the bridges.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your fear is about not being able to get off the bridge if you needed to, rather than the bridge itself?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think it's about being trapped or not being able to leave. If I were stuck in a car somewhere else, like in a normal traffic jam on solid ground, I wouldn't be afraid at all. It really is just the bridge itself... just being up high over all that water. That's what terrifies me, not the idea of being stuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would decrease if you were driving over a bridge that had a shoulder or an emergency lane where you could pull over if needed?\n\nPatient: I don't really think it would make a difference. Even if there was a place to pull over, I'd still be up there on the bridge, over the water. That's the part that scares me. So, no, I don't think having an emergency lane would help at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you were a passenger in a car driving over a bridge, rather than the one driving?\n\nPatient: I don't think it makes a difference. I... I'd still be just as anxious. Whether I'm driving or someone else is behind the wheel, I'm still in the car, up on that bridge. So, yes, I would definitely still be afraid.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious if you were on a bridge, but not in a car, such as if you were walking or biking across it?\n\nPatient: Well, at eighty-six, I certainly wouldn't be biking. And I haven't tried walking across a big bridge in... well, I don't know how long. So I haven't really been in that situation to know for sure. But just thinking about it—being out there in the open, up that high over the water without even the car around me... I imagine I would be just as terrified, if not more so.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of bridges be less if someone else was driving you across?\n\nPatient: I think we actually just touched on that a moment ago, doctor. But no, it wouldn't be any less. Like I was saying, even if someone else is driving me, I'm still stuck in that car, up high over the water. So the fear is exactly the same for me either way.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of bridges be present if you were a passenger on a bus crossing the bridge?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about a bus. I haven't taken one in years, so... I just don't know if that would feel any different for me. I haven't been in that situation to say for certain.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF40.231, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm here because I'm having a lot of trouble with driving lately. Specifically, it's the bridges. What finally made me come in is that the moment my car reaches one of those long bridges toward Miami, my chest just tightens up. My hands clamp down on the steering wheel, and I get this sudden, overwhelming certainty that I have to get off the bridge right then—even though there’s obviously nowhere to go. It just hits me all at once.\n\nDoctor: Is it just bridges that cause this reaction, or are there other situations or places that trigger a similar feeling?\n\nPatient: No, it really just seems to be the bridges. I mean, I drive around town for my errands or to see friends, and I'm perfectly fine. It's only when I have to cross those long stretches over the water. I haven't had that feeling happen anywhere else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you might be having a panic attack when this happens on the bridge?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. I don't know exactly what qualifies as a panic attack, to be honest. I just know that my chest gets tight and I get that overwhelming urge to get away, like I mentioned. But as for whether it's an actual panic attack... I couldn't say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode like the one you described on the bridge in any other situation?\n\nPatient: No, I really haven't. Like I mentioned a moment ago, it's... it's strictly the bridges. I've never felt that sudden tightness or that overwhelming need to escape anywhere else. Whether I'm at home, or just out shopping, I feel completely normal. It only ever happens when I'm driving over the water on those long stretches.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any other situations where you feel like you're having a panic attack?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't. Like I said, it's just the bridges. I don't have those feelings anywhere else, just when I'm driving over the water.\n\nDoctor: Is it the height of the bridges that scares you, or is it more the fact that you're on the bridge itself, regardless of how high it is?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure, to be honest. I haven't really thought about it quite like that. I just know that... well, when I'm out there on the bridge, especially over the water, that's when that feeling hits me. I couldn't really tell you if the height itself is what's doing it, or just the fact that I'm stuck out there on it. I just haven't noticed if a lower bridge feels any different than a high one.\n\nDoctor: Is it the idea of being on a bridge itself that scares you, or is there something else about the situation that you think might be contributing to your fear?\n\nPatient: I think it's just the bridge itself, and being over all that open water. I mean, I know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years. Plenty of people cross them every day without a second thought. But... when I think about driving over them, or when I'm actually doing it, the fear I feel just seems so much bigger than the actual danger. I know I'm safe, logically, but my body just doesn't believe it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel the same level of fear if you were walking on the bridge rather than driving on it?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, honestly. I've never tried walking across one of those bridges... I mean, they're highway bridges, mostly, so you can't exactly walk on them anyway. But even if I could, I think... well, I imagine I'd probably still be just as terrified. It's being out there over the water that gets me, so I don't think it would matter if I was in a car or on my own two feet.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious or trapped in other situations where you couldn't easily escape, such as in a crowded room or on a plane?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't have any trouble with things like that. I've flown on planes and been in crowded rooms plenty of times in my life, and it's never bothered me. I don't feel trapped in those places at all. It really is just the bridges.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your fear is about not being able to get off the bridge if you needed to, rather than the bridge itself?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think it's about being trapped or not being able to leave. If I were stuck in a car somewhere else, like in a normal traffic jam on solid ground, I wouldn't be afraid at all. It really is just the bridge itself... just being up high over all that water. That's what terrifies me, not the idea of being stuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would decrease if you were driving over a bridge that had a shoulder or an emergency lane where you could pull over if needed?\n\nPatient: I don't really think it would make a difference. Even if there was a place to pull over, I'd still be up there on the bridge, over the water. That's the part that scares me. So, no, I don't think having an emergency lane would help at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you were a passenger in a car driving over a bridge, rather than the one driving?\n\nPatient: I don't think it makes a difference. I... I'd still be just as anxious. Whether I'm driving or someone else is behind the wheel, I'm still in the car, up on that bridge. So, yes, I would definitely still be afraid.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious if you were on a bridge, but not in a car, such as if you were walking or biking across it?\n\nPatient: Well, at eighty-six, I certainly wouldn't be biking. And I haven't tried walking across a big bridge in... well, I don't know how long. So I haven't really been in that situation to know for sure. But just thinking about it—being out there in the open, up that high over the water without even the car around me... I imagine I would be just as terrified, if not more so.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of bridges be less if someone else was driving you across?\n\nPatient: I think we actually just touched on that a moment ago, doctor. But no, it wouldn't be any less. Like I was saying, even if someone else is driving me, I'm still stuck in that car, up high over the water. So the fear is exactly the same for me either way.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of bridges be present if you were a passenger on a bus crossing the bridge?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about a bus. I haven't taken one in years, so... I just don't know if that would feel any different for me. I haven't been in that situation to say for certain.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious if you were a passenger on a train crossing a bridge over water?\n\nPatient: To be honest, doctor, I haven't been on a train in... well, probably decades. So it's hard to say. I just don't know if I'd feel that same panic, or if the train being so big and heavy would make a difference. I really haven't been in that situation to give you a certain answer.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I... I'd still be just as anxious. Whether I'm driving or someone else is behind the wheel, I'm still in the car, up on that bridge. So, yes, I would definitely still be afraid.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious if you were on a bridge, but not in a car, such as if you were walking or biking across it?\n\nPatient: Well, at eighty-six, I certainly wouldn't be biking. And I haven't tried walking across a big bridge in... well, I don't know how long. So I haven't really been in that situation to know for sure. But just thinking about it—being out there in the open, up that high over the water without even the car around me... I imagine I would be just as terrified, if not more so.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of bridges be less if someone else was driving you across?\n\nPatient: I think we actually just touched on that a moment ago, doctor. But no, it wouldn't be any less. Like I was saying, even if someone else is driving me, I'm still stuck in that car, up high over the water. So the fear is exactly the same for me either way.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of bridges be present if you were a passenger on a bus crossing the bridge?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about a bus. I haven't taken one in years, so... I just don't know if that would feel any different for me. I haven't been in that situation to say for certain.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious if you were a passenger on a train crossing a bridge over water?\n\nPatient: To be honest, doctor, I haven't been on a train in... well, probably decades. So it's hard to say. I just don't know if I'd feel that same panic, or if the train being so big and heavy would make a difference. I really haven't been in that situation to give you a certain answer.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of bridges be less if you were watching someone else drive across the bridge from a safe location?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't tell you. I don't think I've ever just stood somewhere and watched other people drive across a bridge. I suppose I haven't paid attention to how I'd feel in that exact situation, so I'm just not sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.231, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm here because I'm having a lot of trouble with driving lately. Specifically, it's the bridges. What finally made me come in is that the moment my car reaches one of those long bridges toward Miami, my chest just tightens up. My hands clamp down on the steering wheel, and I get this sudden, overwhelming certainty that I have to get off the bridge right then—even though there’s obviously nowhere to go. It just hits me all at once.\n\nDoctor: Is it just bridges that cause this reaction, or are there other situations or places that trigger a similar feeling?\n\nPatient: No, it really just seems to be the bridges. I mean, I drive around town for my errands or to see friends, and I'm perfectly fine. It's only when I have to cross those long stretches over the water. I haven't had that feeling happen anywhere else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you might be having a panic attack when this happens on the bridge?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. I don't know exactly what qualifies as a panic attack, to be honest. I just know that my chest gets tight and I get that overwhelming urge to get away, like I mentioned. But as for whether it's an actual panic attack... I couldn't say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode like the one you described on the bridge in any other situation?\n\nPatient: No, I really haven't. Like I mentioned a moment ago, it's... it's strictly the bridges. I've never felt that sudden tightness or that overwhelming need to escape anywhere else. Whether I'm at home, or just out shopping, I feel completely normal. It only ever happens when I'm driving over the water on those long stretches.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any other situations where you feel like you're having a panic attack?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't. Like I said, it's just the bridges. I don't have those feelings anywhere else, just when I'm driving over the water.\n\nDoctor: Is it the height of the bridges that scares you, or is it more the fact that you're on the bridge itself, regardless of how high it is?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure, to be honest. I haven't really thought about it quite like that. I just know that... well, when I'm out there on the bridge, especially over the water, that's when that feeling hits me. I couldn't really tell you if the height itself is what's doing it, or just the fact that I'm stuck out there on it. I just haven't noticed if a lower bridge feels any different than a high one.\n\nDoctor: Is it the idea of being on a bridge itself that scares you, or is there something else about the situation that you think might be contributing to your fear?\n\nPatient: I think it's just the bridge itself, and being over all that open water. I mean, I know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years. Plenty of people cross them every day without a second thought. But... when I think about driving over them, or when I'm actually doing it, the fear I feel just seems so much bigger than the actual danger. I know I'm safe, logically, but my body just doesn't believe it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel the same level of fear if you were walking on the bridge rather than driving on it?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, honestly. I've never tried walking across one of those bridges... I mean, they're highway bridges, mostly, so you can't exactly walk on them anyway. But even if I could, I think... well, I imagine I'd probably still be just as terrified. It's being out there over the water that gets me, so I don't think it would matter if I was in a car or on my own two feet.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious or trapped in other situations where you couldn't easily escape, such as in a crowded room or on a plane?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't have any trouble with things like that. I've flown on planes and been in crowded rooms plenty of times in my life, and it's never bothered me. I don't feel trapped in those places at all. It really is just the bridges.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your fear is about not being able to get off the bridge if you needed to, rather than the bridge itself?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think it's about being trapped or not being able to leave. If I were stuck in a car somewhere else, like in a normal traffic jam on solid ground, I wouldn't be afraid at all. It really is just the bridge itself... just being up high over all that water. That's what terrifies me, not the idea of being stuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would decrease if you were driving over a bridge that had a shoulder or an emergency lane where you could pull over if needed?\n\nPatient: I don't really think it would make a difference. Even if there was a place to pull over, I'd still be up there on the bridge, over the water. That's the part that scares me. So, no, I don't think having an emergency lane would help at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you were a passenger in a car driving over a bridge, rather than the one driving?\n\nPatient: I don't think it makes a difference. I... I'd still be just as anxious. Whether I'm driving or someone else is behind the wheel, I'm still in the car, up on that bridge. So, yes, I would definitely still be afraid.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious if you were on a bridge, but not in a car, such as if you were walking or biking across it?\n\nPatient: Well, at eighty-six, I certainly wouldn't be biking. And I haven't tried walking across a big bridge in... well, I don't know how long. So I haven't really been in that situation to know for sure. But just thinking about it—being out there in the open, up that high over the water without even the car around me... I imagine I would be just as terrified, if not more so.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of bridges be less if someone else was driving you across?\n\nPatient: I think we actually just touched on that a moment ago, doctor. But no, it wouldn't be any less. Like I was saying, even if someone else is driving me, I'm still stuck in that car, up high over the water. So the fear is exactly the same for me either way.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of bridges be present if you were a passenger on a bus crossing the bridge?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about a bus. I haven't taken one in years, so... I just don't know if that would feel any different for me. I haven't been in that situation to say for certain.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious if you were a passenger on a train crossing a bridge over water?\n\nPatient: To be honest, doctor, I haven't been on a train in... well, probably decades. So it's hard to say. I just don't know if I'd feel that same panic, or if the train being so big and heavy would make a difference. I really haven't been in that situation to give you a certain answer.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of bridges be less if you were watching someone else drive across the bridge from a safe location?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't tell you. I don't think I've ever just stood somewhere and watched other people drive across a bridge. It's been a very long time since I've been on any kind of boat. I don't know if being down on the water would bother me the same way being up high on the bridge does... I just haven't been in that situation recently enough to tell you how I'd react.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of bridges be present if you were a passenger in a car being driven by someone you trust?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I really couldn't say. I haven't really tested that out to see if it makes a difference. Usually, I just try to avoid going over them entirely, so I don't know if having my son or someone else I trust at the wheel would actually stop me from being afraid. I just don't have a clear answer for you on that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
